- **A second tap turns every block from where it is.** Each block is where the strip was `delay` ago, so it runs on the old way for its own delay and then follows the new leg — its keyframes are resampled from the strip's whole history (`StripSegment`) — and the lean reverses top first, as a wave; no block starts over.
- **A switch whose panes are still loading does not lean** (`loadingInView`). A block React replaces mid-run rides its pane from then on, so a skeleton giving way to its rows during the run jumped by its lag, and at the seam the two panes' lower blocks overlapped by the same amount.
  - So while a first-load placeholder is on screen in either pane, the whole switch slides as one plane. A placeholder counts if it is a `data-page-loading` root: the mark every first-load skeleton root carries for the footer hold.
  - A pane that reads when its tab is first selected therefore leans from its next visit on, once its rows are cached.
  - A new pane's first-load skeleton root needs the mark, or its first switch snaps.

**What the lean costs**, measured on dev against the same switch with the attribute removed: at 1440 the tap's task goes from 19 to 25ms and its first frame comes 3ms later, inside one frame's budget, with 74 → 89 layers mid-switch (twelve blocks). On a phone at 4× CPU the lean's own work in the tap is ~15ms (planning half, animations half) plus ~9ms of its blocks' first style resolution inside React's commit, and the first frame paints ~28ms more, because each block is a stacking context of its own for the run and its content is re-recorded: tap to first frame ~108 → ~137ms, 98 → 111 layers. A run gains one small layout — the restack, in its first frame — and the settle's one forced layout grows, since a block that loses its transform is a containing block no more (14 → ~117 objects, 0.6 → 1.3ms at 1440). The time to 10% of the travel and the speed through a reversal do not move (1440). Standard tier only: 减弱 drops it with the rest of the stagger, 关闭 stays a cut, and an engine without `linear()` gets the strip alone. `npm run net:tabs` samples every element with a box on screen in both panes, every frame of a 图库 ⇄ 论坛 run, and fails if any of them falls more than 45% of the window behind its pane or stands still while it moves — the view row by name — and, on a leaning panel, unless the upper third of the view is at some frame 3% of the window further along than the lower third and reaches halfway across at least 5ms before it (measured at 24ms: 10% and 9–16ms at 1440 and 390; 0 and 0 with the attribute removed). It times the halfway point rather than the first 5% of the travel: the strip leaves fast, so 5% fell inside the first frame or two, and interpolating between frames there read anywhere from 5 to 28ms. The costs above were measured with the 48ms lean; they are the blocks' rather than the delay's, so they stand.

**The strip runs on the compositor.** Each pane is one element with one Web Animations `transform` (`playSharedAxis`), so a frame of the slide costs the main thread nothing: the route's commit, a font slice or an image decode landing mid-run no longer lands on its clock, and a hover hit test under a moving pane finds nothing to lay out. GSAP wrote a transform per frame — on the production build one tap on 论坛 ran 25–35 layouts and ~540 forced style and layout passes inside its run; on dev now 2–3 and 12–13. The tab indicator glides the same way (`lib/slidingIndicator.ts`): a translate between equal widths, a translate and a scale for the thin underline, a width keyframe only for a tall mark whose width really changes.

**The strip is thrown by the tap onto a critically damped spring, not eased along `emphasized` — a stated divergence from MaterialSharedAxis, measured** (`lib/tabStrip.ts`, whose tests in `scripts/testShell.mjs` hold the numbers). Per 60 Hz frame, as a share of the window: `emphasized` over 500ms moves 0.9, 2.9, 5.6, 10.0, 20.7, 23.6 — a tap showed next to nothing for 50ms and then lurched through 60% of the way in the next 50, the slow stretch at the start of a quick switch that read as 阻尼. The strip leaves at twice a switch's mean speed and settles: 9.5, 10.8, 10.9, 10.3, 9.4, 8.3 over 400ms, 10% of the way at 18ms (`emphasized` 52), 90% at 209 (189), settled at 341 (367), and no frame above 11% where `emphasized` puts 24% into one. Critically damped — the effects family, whose normalised rate is 6.65 at every stiffness — because the two sides are adjacent: an overshoot would open a sliver of bare panel past the strip's end, and below a launch of `rate` a critically damped response cannot overshoot, so every launch stays under it. What stays M3's is the shared axis itself — X travel, direction from order, one plane; the full-window strip with no fade is the older, separate divergence argued in `playSharedAxis`. The reduced tier keeps its 24px cross-fade.

**A second tap sends the strip the other way at once, from where it is** (`turnStrip`). Played backwards (`Animation.reverse`), a leg went on the old way for a frame while the engine resolved the new rate (up to a quarter of the window when the tap landed mid-switch), and a late tap retraced the slow end of the curve: 1.1–1.6% of the window per frame for the first 100ms, 10% of the way back 90–140ms after the tap. A turn now starts a new leg from the pose on screen, towards the other side, at a fresh tap's launch speed or the speed it had if that is faster, over `full · clamp(√remaining, 0.6, 1)`: four taps 250ms apart each move 7–11% of the window in the first frame after they land and reach 10% of the way 36–52ms after the tap (dev, 1440). The pose is the old leg's at `performance.now()` — the timeline's time is the last main frame's, which may be long past while the compositor draws on — and when the turn's first frame comes more than a frame and a half after the tap, the leg starts again from that frame, so a late commit cannot open it ahead of the pose on screen. The hero's caught legs keep the flyer's velocity, sign and all, and coast before they turn (`relaunch()`) — right for a picture caught mid-air; for a strip the reader has just sent back, that coast is the same stall again (a velocity-continuous turn 200ms in moves 0.3% of the window in its first frame). An interruption towards a third tab — rows of three or more — still lands the live run and starts afresh.

**Nothing animates the pane box, and the panel's clip is two style writes.** `[data-tab-panel]` used to have its `height` pinned to the outgoing pane's and tweened to the incoming one's over the same 500ms as the slide, so the page's height changed with the motion rather than in one frame at the end of it. Both halves were wrong. The pin was a no-op dressed as a fix: the panel is a grid with both panes in one cell and both hold a box for the run, so its natural height already *is* the taller of the two — all the tween added was a forced shrink to the outgoing height at the start, which is the only reason it needed to clip at all. And what it bought is invisible: the one in-flow element below the panel on every `TabPanes` screen is the shell footer, and the footer carries `data-page-transit` for the tab transit — `opacity: 0`, `transition: none` — on the element itself (`registerPageFooter`, `lib/pageTransit.ts`): keyed from the scroller down to the footer, the rule walked the whole page for its invalidation set at every begin and end of a transit. After `endTransit()` runs in the same `onSettle` as the release, the footer returns on **DefaultEffects, 166ms at default speed, opacity only** — so it is never rendered at a pre-settle position and adds no second travel. On a screen whose panel is the page the content above does not move, because the pre-run clamp against the page's *final* scrollable height guarantees the offset survives the shrink; under shared chrome, the floor below does it.

**Under shared chrome the offset never moves, and neither may the height move it.** On a screen whose panel sits under a header — the gallery's 全部 / 本站讨论 under the banner, a profile — the switch leaves `scrollTop` where it was: the header stays put and the arriving pane starts at its own top. What could still move the page is its height: with the taller pane leaving layout at settle, the offset would sit past the new end and the browser would clamp it — the header thrown down the screen in one frame (本站讨论's height jump). So at settle the panel keeps a floor (`holdFloor`): a `min-height` of exactly the height that keeps the current offset reachable, blank below a short pane down to the bottom of the viewport — what a native tab page shows under a collapsed header. It is written whenever the viewport reaches the panel at all, so a pane whose content lands shorter after the switch cannot pull the page up either, and it is let go on the first scroll that no longer needs it.

The clip survives, because the outgoing pane is translated by `leavingOffsetY` — the difference between the two tabs' remembered offsets, which can be most of a screen — and without it that paints over the footer for the length of the run. It is `overflow-y`, not `overflow`: the panel *is* the centred `max-w-*` content column, and clipping both axes cropped the shared axis to the column for the whole 500ms, so panes appeared and vanished at the text's own edge instead of sliding past the information area's. The value is `clip` rather than `hidden` because `overflow-x: visible` beside `overflow-y: hidden` is *computed to `auto`* by the spec — which would quietly turn the panel into a horizontal scroll container — while `visible` beside `clip` is legal and leaves the x axis alone; the horizontal clip stays where it belongs, on the scroller's `[data-axis-running='x']` rule. It is written with the pane flags rather than after the measurements, so the run costs one style invalidation in the pointer handler instead of two.

`watchPaneGrowth` went with the tween: it was a `ResizeObserver` that re-tweened the same `height` for 2.5 seconds after settle, to absorb the case the tween could not — the height is measured at the moment of the switch, when a pane that fetches on selection still holds its skeleton, so the data landing a beat later moved the height again after the motion had visibly finished. A late change is now an ordinary reflow, like every other data arrival in the app, and `restoreAnchor` puts `overflow-anchor` back at settle so a shrink above the viewport is absorbed by scroll anchoring rather than by a tween on a layout property. Skeletons still have to be the right *length* (`PER_PAGE` rows, not eight) — that requirement got stricter, not looser.

**A page gets a back affordance if, and only if, it is not in the sidebar.** There was no rule, and the distribution showed it: `/search` and `/messages` had one despite being one tap away in the drawer, while `/favorites`, `/history`, `/tasks`, `/block-groups`, `/settings`, `/upload` and `/forum/create` — equally top-level — had none, and `/about` and `/policy`, which are reachable only from the footer and therefore need it most, had none either. So it now sits on exactly the routes with no permanent entry point: both profiles, the forum thread and its edit form (`/forum/[id]/edit`, back to the thread), a subscription's gallery (`/subscriptions/[tag]`, back to the list), `/about`, `/policy` and the image detail. /shop and /subscriptions are drawer rows (金币商店 after 任务, 标签订阅 after 我的收藏, the latter carrying the count of new pictures) and have none. `/forum` looks like it belongs on that list and does not: `next.config.ts` redirects the bare path to `/?tab=forum`, so `app/forum/page.tsx` never mounts and the route you actually land on is the sidebar's own (`/forum/[id]` and `/forum/create` are unaffected — the redirect matches the exact path). Check for a redirect before deciding a route is deep. Draw it in every state, including loading and error: `/derpi/user/[id]` is the cautionary case — its error branch dropped its own 返回上一页 button on the grounds that "the leading back affordance is already chrome on this route", and that route had never rendered one, so a Derpibooru profile that failed to load was a dead end with no way out but the browser's own button.

**The back affordance is chrome, not content.** `PageBack` portals into `[data-page-back-slot]`, a shim the shell renders as a sibling of the scroller, before it — so the control comes before the page in the tab order, as it does on screen. Its label is 返回 with `aria-keyshortcuts="Escape"`, and it and `useEscapeBack` take one handler from `useBackOrParent(parent)` (`lib/backNavigation.ts`): back when there is an in-app entry to return to, otherwise the route's parent — a cold entry must not back out of the app. The page reserves room under it with a class on its root instead of a top padding of its own: `page-back-room` (4xl column), `page-back-room-2xl` (a form column — /claim-badge), `page-back-room-5xl`, `page-back-room-7xl` (a grid column — the favourites' folder pages), each released by a container query once the centred column clears the button, and `page-back-room-media` for a page opening on full-width media, which never is. Rendered inside `[data-page-content]` it was cloned by the route snapshot and translated by the shared axis, so /search → /messages carried it a full window out and back to the pixel it started on. It has no entrance animation on purpose: between two screens that both have one, the node is remounted at the same coordinate looking identical, and a fade would put a flash on every one of those moves. **Between a screen with one and a screen without, it leaves or arrives with its page**: the route snapshot copies the slot (`RouteSnapshot.chrome`), and the cross-fade fades that copy out on the leaving page's clock when the incoming page brings none, or fades the slot in on the arriving page's clock when the leaving page had none — it used to vanish in the frame the route changed and appear a frame ahead of its page. The image detail's own back affordance learned that rule the hard way: it exists twice during a flight — `HeroStage` renders a copy that rides along, `PicDetail` renders the real one — and the handoff hides the first with `visibility` while revealing the second. The route seal used to set `opacity: 0` as well, and `IconButton` carries `opacity` in its transition list (for `disabled:disabled-content`), so lifting the seal started a 200ms 0 → 1 ramp on a glyph that was already on screen a frame earlier: one blink per open. The seal is `visibility` + `pointer-events` only now, which is also strictly better for the sealed copy — `opacity: 0` left it focusable; measured across the handoff, the two copies swap at opacity 1 in the same frame. The riding copy takes `DetailBack`'s `passive`, which is what that prop was added for; without it the app had two focusable 返回图片列表 buttons for the length of every flight. **`data-image-detail-reveal` is on neither copy**, and both used to carry it: their entrance is the `floatingBack` branch of `buildOverlayAnimations`. The routed copy is the dialog's first element — the first Tab stop, as it is the first thing on screen (it followed the section once, after 40-odd tag chips and the comment composer, R10-008) — placed directly in the section, outside the window and its counter-scale, so the container transform never scales it; the Stage's copy precedes its section for the same reason.

**Focus has somewhere to land after a route change.** When a navigation removed the focused element, `AppLayout` moves focus to the incoming page's `<h1>` (or `main` while it has none), never on the document's own load — that page may still be hydrating — and never while the hero or a modal layer owns the screen. It waits for the page to be focusable: the route fade holds it `visibility: hidden` for its opening overlap, where `focus()` silently does nothing.

**Back to a list lands on what was opened from it.** The page being left records itself on every tier (`rememberFolderReturn`, `rememberForumReturn`). For a moment after (`ORIGIN_TTL_MS`), the list marks the card or row it was opened from with `data-return-focus` (`useFolderReturn`, `ForumPostList`), and the landing takes that before the heading. It is focused as itself, so a keyboard sees its ring, and never scrolled to: the restored offset owns the position. While the route's container transform still hides it (`visibility`, for its run), the landing waits for it, up to twice its own bound. A focus resting on `main` counts as lost: an earlier landing put it there when that page had no heading yet.

**A route owns the entrance of the whole incoming page.** `RouteCrossFade` snapshots the outgoing page in `getSnapshotBeforeUpdate`, before React mutates the DOM, and sets `data-route-transit` on the persistent app scroller before the incoming children's layout effects run. `Reveal` and the gallery's `useStaggerGridOn` consult that marker; the grid also latches a skipped entrance so a later update cannot replay it. This keeps the route's motion from stacking with a fade or transform per child. Portalled dialogs outside the page remain independent. `settleEntryAnimations` finishes the page's finite entrance keyframes, leaving perpetual spinners and skeleton shimmer alone.

The plain route fade disables `pageIn` and drives the incoming page's opacity itself. `PAGE_FADE_TIMING` is **80ms of overlap delay plus 320ms of fading**, totaling the same 400ms as the cold entry token. It adds no incoming translation that could corrupt a gallery card's hero measurement. Mapped route moves use the shared axis on the whole page — the same strip; both paths clear the transit marker on completion or cancellation. **The fade is played from the first frame after the commit, not from the commit** (its timeline starts paused and a frame plays it): the commit is a long task, and a clock started inside it spent that task before anything was painted — on a development build the page being left was most of the way gone in the first frame that showed it leaving, and anything started with that frame ran a beat behind the page around it. **Chrome leaves and arrives with its page**: the back affordance (below) and the home pill, whose hide takes the leaving clone's 100ms `accelerate` and whose show takes the arriving page's 80ms + 320ms `decelerate`. **The snapshot keeps its source's query container** (`captureVisualClone`): the page column is one by an attribute the snapshot strips, so the room `page-back-room` reserves came back in the clone and the leaving page dropped by it — 47px at 1440 — in the frame its clone appeared.

**The route footer travels with its page; only tabs hold it aside.** It is already inside `[data-page-content]`, so hiding and re-entering it after a route adds a separate, late arrival. `beginPageTransit` and `data-page-transit` are the tab-only hold, because tab panes sit above the footer and their final height changes at settlement. Their release uses the 166ms DefaultEffects fade above. The other hold is a page's first-load placeholder: while an element marked `data-page-loading` is in the page (outside an inactive tab pane), the footer is `visibility: hidden`, since a skeleton is not the page's length and the footer would otherwise land mid-screen and then be pushed down when the content arrives — mark a screen's first-load skeleton root with it (`app/loading.tsx` and `ImageGridSkeleton` do). **The hold is decided by script, not by the stylesheet** (`lib/pageLoadingHold.ts`: one `data-page-held` attribute on the footer, painted held by the server and released by the footer's ref before the first client paint). It was a `:has()` rule, and Blink invalidated the whole page for it: every flip of a tab pane's active flag restyled both panes — 2,092 elements, 30–65ms on a desktop, 0.1ms with that rule alone stripped — and every insertion or removal in the page, a grid chunk or a placeholder leaving, was a full-page recalc (150–330ms on a throttled phone). Every rewrite that kept `:has()` measured the same. Do not substitute this marker for the route entrance marker: they have different owners and jobs.

**The hero owns the same pixels.** Every other transition stands down while a flight is in progress — gate on `getImageHeroRuntime().phase === 'gallery-idle' && !background`. Inside `lib/motion.ts` that gate is the registered `setHeroBusyCheck` predicate (a seam rather than an import, so `lib/motion` does not drag the hero controller into every bundle); the theme wipe and the tab shared axis both consult it, because the first freezes rendering to snapshot a frame the flyer is moving through and the second sets `overflow-x: clip` on the very scroller that hosts the flight layer.

**A returning hero exposes the gallery to input immediately.** The runtime's `direction` distinguishes both meanings of `reversing`; `AppLayout` removes the gallery's `inert` when it is `back`, before the route commits. It sets it again only once the detail has **settled** (`detail-idle`): `inert` on the gallery's `<main>` restyles every card under it (1,450 elements, up to 66ms on a desktop), so it must not flip inside a flight's frames — the intercepted route commits mid-flight on an open, and a close turned back toward the detail used to re-inert the gallery at the instant it reversed, taking the head of the reversed leg with it. The close itself waits two frames before it measures and flies: the first carries that restyle, so the leg's head is presented rather than spent behind it. **The leaving route is frozen before those frames**, in the same task as the phase that lifts the gallery's `inert`: frozen after them, the detail was still a live modal layer for two frames, and its focus trap pulled a focus the user had just given the gallery back into the detail (a full production run caught it once; `return-focus` now focuses inside the very mutation that lifts the `inert`). The leaving surfaces take no pointer input through `data-image-hero-leaving`, set by the engine on each surface a `back` leg is leaving — not a flag on `<html>` under a universal descendant rule, which Blink invalidates by the attribute's *name* and so turned every open and close into a style pass over the whole document (R12-009). Keeping `inert` until the detail route unmounted sent a new wheel/touch stream into the non-scrollable host, and the close then waited for that same stream to stop. `HeroScrollContinuity` distinguishes the visible input target from the peer that last scrolled: fresh input drops old residuals before the browser's default scroll, and proves the handoff can finish without waiting for the new stream to go quiet. `setInputTarget` reattaches a peer that an outgoing event removed during the reveal frame. The closing route and its sibling back button share one inert lease; its eventual focus restore preserves any new focus the user chose in the gallery. Quiet, expiry and abort are explicit outcomes: a still-owned, non-aborted transfer proceeds on budget expiry instead of comparing a timer against a fractional deadline, which browsers can fire just before.

**An interrupted hero keeps the rendered clock and pose.** Read WAAPI `currentTime` for interruption; fades and the header's in-flight rise start from their current values. A viewport rebuild reschedules the shared tracks on the same remaining clock unless a pull gesture already owns them. Reverse destinations remain the real host/card, even after a rebase replaced the leg's start with an intermediate pose. A continued background scale keeps its original pivot while below 1, or scrolling changes the pivot and produces a translation jump. The recognizer coalesces pull samples once; the surface writes that frame directly rather than queuing a second frame. `npm run test:hero` covers native wheel/touch at return boundaries, delayed-data Esc + scroll, reopens, focus, and interruption — and the viewer's own navigation: 上一张 / 下一张 by key, button and swipe with the history left as it was, a second flick or key press taken over mid-step (the swipe's in both halves, pose and fade continuous) with the ladder still naming the picture on screen, a video paged by a swipe but not from its controls band, a flick taken over as the swap lands on a video, a drag at an end whose next page is still loading, a long run of varied swipes over slow reads that ends at rest after every release, Back at five points of a step's ladder rewrite (the swap's task, the frame after, inside the rewrite's own step onto the base on a busy main thread and an idle one, and past the base once it has written) closing to the list's own entry every time, Back and Forward after stepping, a step across a page and the close that lands on that page's card — focus with it, flown or flightless — the lightbox and a dialog each peeled by Back before the viewer, a reload's collapse, a cold entry's 返回, a tap turning a return around, and the last flyer frame on both legs within half a pixel of its destination at 1440, 1920 and 390 with a classic scrollbar's gutter in play; `npm test` also exercises the pure continuity/velocity contracts.

And nothing may leave a residual `transform` on an ancestor of a gallery card: the flight reads `getBoundingClientRect` on press, and only a transform on `[data-image-detail-background-visual]` is compensated for. Always settle with `clearProps`, never `translate3d(0,0,0)`. This is easy to breach from a distance — `pageIn` was a fade *plus a 12px rise* under a `both` fill, and `[data-page-content]` is an ancestor of every card, so for as long as the plain route cross-fade relied on that keyframe to bring the new page in, arriving at `/` from any route outside `ROUTE_CELL` left a ~400ms window in which a tap launched the flyer from a box up to 12px above the thumbnail. The plain branch now drives the entrance itself, on opacity alone, and `pageIn` is opacity-only as well.

**The gallery has no blurs to stand down any more, and that is the fix rather than the workaround.** `Badge tone="media"` carried a small backdrop blur and three of those ride every gallery thumbnail, so a 50-card grid held on the order of 150 backdrop-filter regions — each re-sampling what is behind it on every frame the grid moved, and the grid moves in the hero flight, the tab shared axis and every route cross-fade. Measured on presented compositor frames: 28fps with them live, 31fps without. Three CSS rules used to suppress `backdrop-filter` inside the moving subtree per transition; the blur is gone from the tone instead, so the cost is gone everywhere rather than suppressed in three places, and the legibility figure that justifies the plate (4.8:1 for a pure-white subject under black at 55%) never included the blur anyway. The tombstone is in globals.css where the rules were.

**Chrome that appears only on one side of the handoff is chrome that jumps.** The detail's zoom control uses `hover-reveal`: visible on compact and touch screens, hidden at rest only from `sm` on a device with a fine pointer and hover, and revealed by hover or keyboard focus. `HeroStage`'s landing target renders no children at all — so on touch the frame that hands the picture over conjured a 40dp button into its corner, and the frame that starts a close took it away. `html[data-image-hero-transition] [data-image-detail-zoom]` stands it down for the length of a flight. Anything else added inside the media box needs the same treatment or a twin on the Stage.

**A viewport change mid-flight has to re-aim the container, not just the flyer.** `rebuild()` did only the flyer for a long time, and the window is a pose expressed against the *host's* box — so when the host resized, the same numbers landed somewhere else and the keyframes were still aimed at the old box. On a phone the trigger is the address bar collapsing. Measured at 400px wide with the flight 90ms in and the height changed by 60px: the flyer moved 19px in that frame while the mask jumped **682 → 784**, then converged on the pre-resize host and finished 8px short of the new one; `rebuildContainer` rebases it from wherever it is toward a freshly measured host, and after the fix the same test converges on 791 against a host of 792. Re-reading the host is safe because the overlay is the one node in that chain that never carries a transform — which is also why `createPlane` takes it as the plane's origin rather than the scroller, whose rect *is* the scaled box once a leg is running. A one-frame jump of a full-screen mask is the artefact that reads worse the lower the refresh rate, because it *is* the difference between two adjacent frames — do not reach for the frame rate when that is the complaint.

**A route with no media has to say so.** The handoff waits for a route with a paintable preview *and* a target, and only `DetailImage`/`DetailVideo` set either — so the failure branch could never satisfy it and the wait ran to `HERO_DETAIL_ROUTE_TIMEOUT_MS`: an image that turned out not to exist left the error page sealed and the screen blank for **30 seconds**. `resolvedWithoutMedia` on the registration is the terminal answer that lets the container transform finish and the error surface in its place; the closing path already fell back to a plain history collapse when `route.target` is null.

**上一张 / 下一张 step in place, inside the ladder — never a navigation** (decision 19, R10-001/002). The list a picture was opened from hands the viewer its order (`lib/imageSequence.ts`: `openFromSequence` on activation, `useImageNeighbours` in the detail); the viewer steps through it with the arrows, ←/→ and a horizontal swipe on the picture, loading the list's next page at an end, and a picture opened from nowhere has no pair at all. A step is **one tree**: the route never remounts and the router is never called. `imageHeroHistory.retarget` rewrites the ladder behind it — the guard steps back onto the base, the base's URL is replaced through the native `history.replaceState` path Next documents (the URL and `usePathname` change, the tree does not), and a fresh guard is pushed with the same token — so stepping never adds an entry, Back still closes the viewer to the list, and a reload names the picture on screen. The router route was the defect: `router.replace` dispatched inside a popstate listener landed before Next's own `ACTION_RESTORE`, which discarded it (the popstate waiters now resolve in a task after the dispatch, `afterDispatch`), and even without the race the flow overwrote the gallery entry and stranded a stale ladder forward of it.

**A Back can overtake the rewrite, and then the rewrite writes nothing.** Its own step onto the base is a traversal, and it writes only after that pop's dispatch (`afterDispatch`); a Back the browser takes meanwhile is resolved against the entry history is heading for — the provisional rung under the base — and on a busy main thread its commit queues behind the rewrite's, so Chromium dispatches both popstates before the rewrite's continuation (every time, measured). Written anyway, the base's new URL went onto the entry the Back had reached, the list's own: the list on screen under `/pic/<id>`, a guard pushed over it, a reload opening the picture again — the production build did exactly that. So `retarget` writes only if no pop has landed since its own and the base is still current, and a step the user's traversal abandoned passes its signal into the rewrite, not just into the wait (`replaceUnownedDetail`, for a viewer without a ladder, checks that the entry it replaces is still the picture's). A traversal that lands on that provisional rung from the base or the guard — ahead of the writes or after them, or a history-menu jump of two entries — is a close the router has already made: the list follows the viewer to the picture last on screen and the rung is stepped off, or the next Back would land on the list's own entry at the same URL and seem to do nothing. One thing no page can fix: a Back that names its target before the rewrite's step and lands after it — Playwright's `page.go_back()` reads the history and navigates by entry id, and `navigation.back()` resolves in the page — names the same entry as the rewrite, and the browser makes one traversal of the two; the Back is absorbed and the viewer stays on a consistent URL. A browser's own Back is resolved against the pending entry and never is, which is why the suite's Back is `history.back()`.

**The picture on screen is `useDetailStep`'s, not the route's `[id]` and not the URL.** The tree keeps naming the picture the viewer opened on, the URL lags the swap by a traversal, and the swap lags the press by the outgoing half of the transition; the location is adopted whenever no step is rewriting it (Back, a forward replay, a reload). Per-picture state resets on the swap, because the route no longer remounts to reset it. And the ladder's rewrite waits for the swap to land and an idle slice (`STEP_SYNC_IDLE_TIMEOUT_MS`), or runs at once when a close needs it: its two router commits re-render every location subscriber — the list under the viewer included — and beside the swap they delayed the outgoing half 200ms after the press (measured in development); a run of presses rewrites once, for the picture it stops on.

**The transition is M3's shared axis X, sequentially, in one tree.** `MaterialSharedAxis` fades the outgoing view out before the incoming one fades in, so no instant shows both and nothing needs a clone of the page: out on FastEffects sliding 30dp toward the side it leaves (8px reduced), one `flushSync` swap while nothing is visible, in on DefaultSpatial/DefaultEffects from the other side; off is a cut. The overlay scroller returns to the top on the swap. A swipe follows the finger (rubber-banded past an end), leaves at the speed it was released with, and springs home on FastSpatial when it does not page; the picture's `touch-action` leaves `pan-x` out so the browser never claims the drag, while the pull-down and the pinch keep theirs. A video pages too, except from the band along its bottom edge, where its own controls take a horizontal drag for the timeline; it used to take no swipe at all, and the first video in a list stopped every swipe after it until an arrow was pressed. At an end whose next page is still loading, the drag rubber-bands as at a true end and a release that would page joins that read — one picture, once the page lands — rather than being refused for as long as the read takes. ←/→ step only while the viewer has the keyboard: no modifier, no IME composition, not in a field, not in a control that owns the arrows. **A second flick is never dropped for arriving mid-step** — a native pager takes a new drag from its in-flight pose, and so does this one (`takeOverStepMotion`): during the incoming half the travel stops where it is and becomes the drag's origin while the fade runs on; during the outgoing half the swap lands at once and the finger takes the incoming picture from its first pose. The recogniser used to refuse a finger until the step had settled, which the production build turned into a lost flick: its idle slice, and with it the URL, lands inside the incoming half, where a flick timed off the URL arrived. ←/→ and the arrows have no such gate: in the incoming half a press leaves from the in-flight pose, in the outgoing half it retargets the swap still ahead (the latest press wins). **A released drag always ends at rest.** Taking over the outgoing half lands its swap first, and that swap can unbind the recogniser — it did when the incoming picture was a video — whose teardown releases the drag; so the takeover checks the finger is still down before it pins a pose. Pinned without one, the picture stayed 30px off centre with nothing left to move it: "stuck halfway".

**The arrows are the dialog's trailing actions**, top-right opposite the back affordance and in its square tonal shape, pinned like it. Riding on the picture they moved with every box, so a run of presses chased the button across the screen; pinned, the pointer stays on it whatever the next picture's size. They stand down during a flight (the Stage draws none) and fade with the pull (`data-image-detail-chrome`). At a true end an arrow is unavailable, and it shows the wait while the next page loads.

**Closing after stepping lands on the picture on screen.** Before the return measures, `revealInImageSequence` asks the list to bring that card into view — turning its own page under the overlay if the steps crossed one (the list writes its address back once its own entry is current) — bounded by `STEP_REVEAL_TIMEOUT_MS`, after which the close is flightless rather than aimed off screen. A viewer opened without a flight reveals too, without waiting for it. **Focus follows the reveal, not the overlay's return.** The overlay's own return (`returnFocus`, the card in the active pane) can run before the list has turned its page — always in a flightless close — and then falls back to the card the viewer was opened from, which the page turn unmounts, leaving focus on the document. So once a reveal resolves and the detail has gone, the controller hands focus to the revealed card without scrolling (the reveal owns the position), and only while focus is lost: a choice made in the list meanwhile keeps it.

**A reload with the viewer open keeps the ladder in history with nothing behind it.** The page presentation's 返回 collapses it to the list it was opened from (`leaveImageHeroOrphanLadder` → `collapseOrphanMarker`, the entry the user came from with its search and page), and a cold entry with no ladder goes back through history when the entry behind is the app's, otherwise up to `/` in place — never a push (R10-003 / R4-041).

**Forward pressed during a closing flight is lost, and that is the browser's, not a bug to chase** (R10-010). The close collapses the ladder with one traversal; a `history.forward()` queued behind it is coalesced away by the browser before any popstate reaches the page, so there is nothing to replay. Forward after the close has landed replays the open as usual.

**The list's offset under the viewer is remembered per viewer entry** (the Navigation API's entry key), so leaving the viewer for another route and coming back with Back restores the list the router remounts under it (R12-017). It is read in an idle slice after a landing or a step, not at the landing itself — reading `scrollTop` there forced a layout of the whole page (77ms on a first open in development) — which is safe because the covered, inert list cannot move in between.

**Measure presented frames, not `requestAnimationFrame` gaps.** *The figures in this paragraph predate the curve, bow and single-clock pass and have not been reproduced since; re-measure before quoting them.* A composited animation keeps running while the main thread is busy, so rAF intervals report main-thread cadence and say nothing about what the display got — the two disagreed by a factor of two here. A CDP screencast (`Page.startScreencast`, `everyNthFrame: 1`) yields one event per presented frame, and a full-viewport composited transform on an otherwise idle page is the control that proves the harness can reach 60. What that showed: the flight presents at **36fps** against 60 idle, and **no single animated track is responsible** — cancelling the flyer's corner morph, the container mask, the content fit and every skeleton shimmer, together, was worth about one frame per second. The cost is the detail route's first render and first paint landing inside the flight window (~190ms of React work in a production build), spread evenly across it; the blur rule above was the only change that moved the number (27 → 36).

**The ~190ms attribution has now been re-measured and it does not hold — do not act on it.** Measured on a production build against a 50-card gallery, four opens, `Performance.getMetrics` deltas between the tap and `opening.landed` (medians): `ScriptDuration` **50ms**, `RecalcStyleDuration` **54ms**, `LayoutDuration` **3.6ms**, `TaskDuration` **220ms**, over a tap-to-landed wall time of **384ms**; between `landed` and `detail-idle` the same counters read 3ms / 11ms / 0.4ms. So the route's commit *is* inside the window, but it is a fifth of the window's cost rather than three quarters of it — style recalculation is the same size, and `TaskDuration` exceeds script plus style plus layout by more than double, which puts the remainder in paint and raster. Two consequences. **Deferring the route's chrome to `opening.landed` is not worth it**: it would move part of 50ms out of a window that is spending 220ms, in exchange for the picture landing and the metadata arriving as two separate events — the failure this file names repeatedly — plus a real risk to the header-height contract the handoff depends on; it was planned and then dropped on this measurement. **And the leg is not the whole flight**: 250ms of `HERO_DURATIONS` against 384ms tap-to-landed means roughly 130ms is spent before the flyer moves, which is where `frameCache`'s press-path warming and the cache count now aim.

One caveat on the harness: a headless *and* a headed Chromium on this machine both cap the presented-frame control at 46fps, so presented frame rate could not be measured here at all — only main-thread occupancy, which is machine-independent. The 36fps figure above still needs a device with a real compositor to re-confirm.

## State layers

Hover/focus/press are the `state-layer` utility — a tinted overlay at the M3 alpha, painted from the element's own `color`. Not `hover:bg-primary/90`, which must be written twice (light + dark) and drifts.

Hover requires `(hover: hover)`, matching Tailwind's variants; a touch tap must not leave a permanent hover tint. Keyboard focus remains independent of that query, including the gallery's media scrim. Selection controls use named groups so a surrounding card's hover cannot light up an untouched checkbox or radio. Ripple hosts stay mounted when disabled so an existing wave retains its clipping; the delegated press gate checks native `:disabled` (including fieldsets) and `aria-disabled` instead.

The four alphas are M3's, from `StateTokens`: **hover .08, focus .10, pressed .10, dragged .16**. Focus and pressed are deliberately *equal* — the focus ring is what distinguishes them, not the tint weight.

**A ripple is that same layer spreading from the point of contact**, so it holds one opacity for its whole life and then fades. Its value lives on `.ripple` in globals.css and reads the pressed token; `spawnRipple` drives only the scale and the fade-out.

**Its fade is one keyframe, and that is a rule rather than a shorthand.** A WAAPI keyframe list is *absolute*, where `gsap.to(el, { opacity: 0 })` starts from whatever the element currently is — `[{ opacity: 1 }, { opacity: 0 }]` does not mean "fade out from here". Written that way, with the fade's 300ms delay and no `backwards` fill, the wave jumped from the token's 0.10 to **1** at the instant the animation became in-effect and then fell: a full-strength `currentColor` flash at the end of every press. A single keyframe at offset 1 takes an **implicit start from the underlying value**, which is the token. The test for the next conversion: this is the only animated element whose **CSS sets a non-default opacity** and the only fade with a **delay and no `backwards` fill** — `Toast`, `Reveal`, `Popover` and the hero's retire fade all start from a CSS opacity of 1. `npm run perf:ripple` samples it per frame.

A **selection control needs its own state layer** and cannot use this utility. `Checkbox`, `Radio` and `ToggleSwitch` each paint a real 40dp circle instead: the utility keys on the element's own `:hover` and covers only that element, while what has to light up on a checkbox is a circle more than twice the width of the 18dp box, driven by a hover anywhere on its label.

**A roving cursor is `state-layer-active`.** `state-layer` keys on the element's own `:hover`/`:focus-visible`/`:active` and misses the one state a roving cursor creates — a row that is the keyboard's current item without being focused itself (focus is on the control, `aria-activedescendant` names the row). `state-layer-active` paints the same layer unconditionally at the focus weight, which is what the state is; `Select`'s listbox and /search's autocomplete both have exactly that cursor. The cursor is not the *selection*: a chosen value takes the container pair `secondary-container` — what "selected" means everywhere in this app (the sidebar's route, a chosen `Select` option, the current contact), and under 多色 the theme's second colour; a selected `Chip` takes the `chip-selected` pair, the same thing in 单色's single hue, because an accent never wears a chip. A cursor row must not take `primary-container`, which says "committed" about a row the user has not committed to.

## Layout

Spacing is the 4dp grid (Tailwind's default scale). Half-steps (`gap-1.5`, `py-0.5`) are fine for dense chrome; arbitrary values are not.

**The page column is decided by what is in it, and there are two answers.**

| Content | Width |
| --- | --- |
| A list of rows, or an article | `max-w-4xl` |
| A masonry / image grid | `max-w-7xl` |

Twelve screens are lists — forum, messages, history, tasks, policy, about, settings, block-groups, tag-groups, subscriptions and both forum sub-pages — and they all sit at `4xl`. /block-groups and /tag-groups are one grouped run of rows each, drawn by one row (`components/groups/GroupRow.tsx`), not a grid of cards. Three are grids and sit at `7xl`, and so do the two that came with the shop and the subscriptions: /shop (a grid of items, its cart docked beside them from `@4xl`) and a subscription's gallery (`/subscriptions/[tag]`). Nothing else is a page column. The trap is a screen that holds both: the home page's two tabs share one panel, and a pane whose content is a list takes the list width regardless of what its neighbour needs.

A form is neither, and may be narrower — `/upload` is `2xl` on purpose (a 560px field column is easier to fill in than a 900px one); the admin console is `6xl` because it is data tables. `5xl` is the fifth and last: a **media-led detail column** — both profile pages and the image detail, where a banner or a picture is the subject and the text sits under it. It is not a general-purpose middle width; a screen that is a list still takes `4xl` even if it happens to be 1000px of list.

**A block inside a column is not a column**, and this is where a sixth width comes from. A block takes the width of whatever it *is*, and the answer is one of the five; if it is not, that is the signal the block is being sized by eye. (/search is a `7xl` page whose field and advanced-filter panel are a *form*, so both take the form column's `2xl` — not a `3xl` that is on no list.)

**A page has one gutter and the shell already draws it.** `[data-page-content]` is `p-4 sm:p-6`, so a screen that wraps its sections in another `p-6` insets them 40px on a phone and 48px on a desktop — invisible in review because nothing looks broken, the column is just narrower than every other screen's. A section is a heading and the block under it; the block's own tone (`.m3-row`, `Card`) is what makes it a surface.

**A structural divider is drawn in every state.** If a rule separates two regions of the drawer, it does not blink on and off with the contents of one of them — signed out the top block is still a block, it just says 未登录. (The rule *inside* the nav, between 我的 and the settings group, is a different thing and stays conditional: signed out there is no group above it.)

**A row has one reading order.** Label at the leading edge, control at the trailing edge — that is what M3's list is. A switch dropped into such a list *without* `layout="row"` renders control-then-label and leaves the right-hand half of the row empty. /settings holds this with one anatomy, `SettingsRow` (`app/settings/SettingsRow.tsx`). The label column is `min-w-0 flex-1`, so a long value wraps inside it and the control stays trailing at every width; below `sm`, an action collapses to its glyph (`RowButton`) instead of dropping under the label. There is one type pairing, `label-l` over `body-s`, and the leading slot is for media only. The row wears no state layer, because it is not a control: a row that lit up on hover answered nothing, and a switch row lit twice.

**A header's shape is decided by its column, never by a number in it.** /shop's actions (the balance and 金币明细) are one block, as wide as the column (`100cqw`) below the shop column's md step and beside the title from it. Under 21rem the balance's words go to the glyph and to a screen reader. Laid out by its content, one more digit re-wrapped the header and the page jumped 44 px under the leaving sheet. A screen with a figure in its header actions does the same, or `PageHeader` gains a breakpoint for it.

**A grouped list is one run of `.m3-row` siblings per parent.** The last row's corner is `:nth-last-child(1 of .m3-row)`; the sibling `:has()` it replaced was re-matched on every inline style the tab wave (since removed) wrote to a forum row (a trace of one 图库 → 论坛 switch counted 327 `:has()` invalidations on those rows). Every parent in the app holds exactly one run; a second run needs a parent of its own.

**A pane split follows its content width.** Search filters and blocking groups use container queries; private messages need 42rem for an 18rem contact list and a 24rem conversation. Below that, contacts and the thread share one column, including on a tablet with the app drawer open — and there an open conversation is a **full-height view of its own**: portalled into the content host beside the scroller (the page column is a size container, so nothing fixed can escape it), a modal layer with its own history entry, so the system Back closes it back to the list (`useOverlayLayer` with `history`). JS decides which, by measuring the column, because the choice is whether a layer exists. The contact-collapse preference survives resizing and only applies while both panes fit. The message composer is a deliberate 48dp dense field (28px line + 10px padding per side), with the same local 48dp floor on its accessories; ordinary form fields remain 56dp. It is an editable box in the filled field shell rather than a textarea (`ChatComposer`), so a picked emoji is the picture itself and not its `$emoji_…$` token; it grows with its content to a 160px cap and then scrolls, and it keeps its own undo history, because the engine's cannot see an insertion the page made.

### The control-height scale

**Two rules, and the second one is the one that gets forgotten.** The step set is 32 / 40 / 56 with nothing between — every step is a container height some token actually names, which is the whole point of writing it down (eight heights were once in use because nothing stated one). But a step set alone does not decide anything — that is how a 56dp dropdown ended up in a row whose other three controls were 32dp switches.

**Rule 1 — the touch floor is a *touch* floor.** M3's 48dp is WCAG 2.5.5's AAA figure and it answers a finger. A pointer's floor is WCAG 2.2 **SC 2.5.8 — 24×24 CSS px**, at AA. `--touch-floor` in globals.css is 48px and drops to 24px under `@media (pointer: fine)`; `touch-target` (a hit area, no layout change) and `touch-size` (a real `min-height`/`min-width`, for a control whose own box clips a pseudo-element away — `data-ripple` does, unless the wave paints into an inner host; see the touch-target note) both read it. Applying 48 to a mouse is not a free margin: `state-layer` keys on `:hover`, so a region wider than the paint lights the control up while the cursor is visibly outside it, and neighbours in a dense row start answering for each other. Two `@custom-variant`s, `pointer-coarse:` and `pointer-fine:`, express the axis at a call site; `MEDIA.pointerCoarse` / `MEDIA.pointerFine` are the JS twins so the two cannot drift.

Do not reach for `touch-target` on an inline target inside prose — SC 2.5.8 exempts one, and a 48px band on a line in a 28px-leading paragraph steals clicks from the lines above and below.

**Rule 2 — a control's step is decided by its enclosure, not by its type.** This is what "coordinated" means, and it is checkable: look at what the control's neighbours are, not at what the control is called.

| Enclosure | Step | Text |
| --- | ---- | ---- |
| A form column — a slot you fill in | 56 | `body-l` |
| The dense chat composer's field | 48 | `body-l` |
| The chat composer's emoji and send controls | 48, through the row's local touch floor | — |
| A filter bar, an admin surface, or inside an `.m3-row` | 40 | `body-m` for typed text; `label-l` for choices and commands |
| An app-bar action | 48 | — |
| Dense chrome: a table row's actions, a chip's dismiss cross | 32 | `label-l` |

The composer is the stated dense exception: its 28px prose line plus 10px block padding on each side makes a 48dp field. The row sets a local 48dp touch floor so both accessories match it at every pointer density. With 8dp around the trio, the one-line composer is 64dp tall; growing text increases the field until its scrolling cap. Ordinary form fields retain their 56dp baseline.

**The app bar sets its own 48dp floor**, so every `touch-size` inside its `<header>` lands at 48 whatever the pointer is. Those controls sit on a 64dp coloured band. The dense chat composer separately sets the same local floor to match its field; neither changes the global pointer-dependent floor for other controls.

**A row is a different kind of object from a control** (M3 separates them too — `ListTokens` has its own heights). Row heights take the density step:

| Row | pointer | touch |
| --- | ---- | ---- |
| Menu row, `Select` option row | 40 | 48 |
| Drawer navigation row | 48 | 56 |
| Two-line list item (`/messages`' contact row) | 72 | 72 |
| The drawer's account block | 64 | 64 |

The contact row is `ItemTwoLineContainerHeight` at **every** density; the density step was tried on it and taken back out, because the collapsed contact rail's width is *derived* from that height (row height + the list's 8px inset either side, which makes the row square so the 48dp portrait centres itself at the row's 12px padding) — a row three other numbers depend on is the wrong place to spend a step.

The account block is **64 with `ListTokens`' own 40dp avatar**, deliberately *not* a two-line list item even though it has two lines: it is the drawer's header, above sixteen 48dp links. 64 is one step down from the 72 list-row height with the spec's avatar intact, leaving 12px above and below the text stack — exactly M3's own item padding. 56 with a 32dp avatar made the one portrait in the shell the smallest one in the app (32 is the *dense* step, for a chat turn). Its name is `title-s`, matching the `label-l` size of every link below it.

**The page-content wrapper is a flex column, and `[&>*]:w-full` goes with it.** The column exists so `StatusView`'s `fill` can be `flex-1`. The `w-full` is not decoration: a flex item in a column is stretched on the cross axis by `align-items: stretch` **unless it has an auto margin there** — an auto cross-axis margin absorbs the free space and disables the stretch. Every page root in this app is `mx-auto max-w-*`, so without it all of them fall back to shrink-to-fit. If you touch that wrapper, measure a **width** on a tab switch, not just a height.

**48 is not a general-purpose control size.** It is the touch floor and the pointer-side drawer row, plus the explicitly dense chat composer above. Do not turn that local exception into a second ordinary form-field height.

| Step | What takes it |
| ---- | --- |
| 32 | `Button size="xs"`, `IconButton size="sm"`, `Chip` |
| 40 | the default: `Button`, `IconButton`, `Select size="sm"`, `Input size="sm"`, a menu row, `Pagination`, a control beside a field |
| 48 | a tab (`PrimaryNavigationTabTokens.ContainerHeight`), an app-bar action, the dense chat composer and its accessories, the drawer row under a pointer, and `--touch-floor` under a finger |
| 56 | `Button size="lg"`, a labelled or unlabelled `Input`, the search bar, `Select`, `ColorSwatch`, `CodeInput`, the drawer row under a finger |
| 64 | the top app bar (`AppBarSmallTokens.ContainerHeight`, at every density — the band is not repeated chrome, so the density rule does not apply to it); the drawer's account block |
| 72 | a two-line list item (`ListTokens.ItemTwoLineContainerHeight`), at every density |

**A 40dp field is this app's own step, not the spec's** (`OutlinedTextFieldTokens.ContainerHeight` is 56 and M3 offers nothing else). A field in a filter bar or a list row is *chrome*: its neighbours are a 40dp dropdown and a 32dp chip, and matching them matters more than matching a token written for a phone form. Two fences keep it from spreading — it is unlabelled-only (the labelled field's notch geometry is all derived from 56dp) and its `trailing` slot takes a single 32dp control, on the same inset formula as every size, `(40 − 32) / 2`. `SearchInput` bakes it in rather than offering it, because every call site of that component is a filter.

Three figures went: **28** is below M3's smallest button; **36** sat between extra-small 32 and small 40, on no scale; **44** is Apple's touch figure rather than Material's, this app's own invention.

One consequence: **the two ordinary text fields are the same height.** Their boundary and corner differ, as described in the Input section; both remain 56dp in a form. The dense steps belong to their own enclosures and do not change that form baseline.

`Button size="sm"` is gone rather than retuned: at 40dp it was `md`, and two names for one box is the problem, not the fix. Its 32 call sites moved to `xs`, because every one had been chosen to be *smaller* than the default and that intent maps to M3's extra-small.

Button padding is **16 / 16 / 24** by size and the icon gap is 8 at every size — `IconLabelSpace` does not vary in the token set, which is why a 32dp button and a 56dp one put the same air between glyph and label. `ButtonXSmallTokens.LeadingSpace` is 16dp, the same as small's; only the medium step widens.

The **glyph size belongs to the button**, not to the call site: `IconSize` is 20 at extra-small and small, 24 at medium, and both `Button` and `IconButton` size their own icon slot.

**A press does not morph the corner**, and the spec does specify that it should (`ButtonSmallTokens.PressedContainerShape` is `CornerSmall` (8dp), `ButtonMediumTokens`' is `CornerMedium` (12dp)) — both were implemented and came out anyway; they were animated `border-radius` only, deliberately not a transform. On a pill the corner has nowhere to travel *to* that reads as feedback. Press feedback is the state layer and the ripple, which is what every other control in the app uses. A **selected** icon button is still a rounded square rather than a circle, per `SelectedContainerShapeRound` — that is a state, not a press.

### The icon-size scale

**18 / 20 / 24 / 36 / 48**, through `ICON` in `lib/icons.ts` — `size={ICON.standard}`, never a number.

| Name | dp | What takes it |
| --- | -- | --- |
| `dense` | 18 | inside a chip, or beside a line of metadata |
| `control` | 20 | inside a button or a dense icon button |
| `standard` | 24 | the default: a list row, a nav row, an app-bar action, a field adornment |
| `large` | 36 | a large FAB's glyph, or one prominent affordance |
| `display` | 48 | an illustration: the glyph over an empty state or an error |

Below 18 a standalone Material Symbol stops resolving clearly. **Supporting marks have explicit primitive-owned exceptions**: `Badge` uses 14 at `sm` and 16 at `md`; `Select` draws its 16dp light `CheckGlyph` inside a reserved 20dp slot. These marks accompany text and are not separate targets. The badge's line box is 16px, so an 18dp glyph would make a badge with an icon 2px taller than one without; the listbox's reserved slot similarly keeps selection from moving its label.

`Avatar` and `SkeletonCircle` are **not** on this scale: they take a box size, not a glyph size. An avatar's steps are 32 / 40 / 48 / 56, with 40 the default because that is `ListTokens`' leading-avatar size.

### The slider does not look like the old slider

`Slider`'s geometry is M3 Expressive's (`SliderTokens` v2_3_5) and the instinct on seeing it is to "fix" it back: the track is **16dp** tall, not 4, and the handle is a **4dp-wide vertical pill 44dp tall**, not a 20dp circle. Two details carry most of the character and both look like bugs if you do not know they are the spec:

- **The handle sits in a 6dp gap.** The track is visibly *cut* on both sides of it rather than passing behind it — that gap is what makes a thick track read as two segments with a position between them instead of a progress bar with a lump on it.
- **The handle narrows on press**, 4dp to 2dp. Not grows. M3 gives no control size feedback that *adds* mass on press, and a handle that swells under the finger hides the value it is reporting.

Nothing about the position is transitioned, also deliberately: a slider reports where the input is, and a transition on the fill or the handle puts the mark behind the finger for the whole drag. The only animated property is the handle's width, which is a state rather than a position.

**The painted track spans the handle's travel, not the box.** The native thumb's centre only travels from half its width to the far end less half its width, and the handle is drawn there; a track painted edge to edge left an active stub at the minimum with the handle standing 22px in, so the minimum read as above it (and the maximum mirrored it). **Focus is on the handle**, as M3 draws it — the ring used to wrap the whole 44dp-tall control in a pill.

Touch targets: use a primitive and the question does not arise — `Button` (every size), `IconButton`, `Chip` (its label and its cross), `Select`'s trigger and `Tabs` reach `--touch-floor` themselves. Anything with a custom box under it — 48px under a coarse pointer (M3's minimum and WCAG 2.5.5's AAA figure), 24px under a fine one (WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8 at AA) — needs `touch-target`, which grows a pseudo-element by negative insets to the floor on whichever axis falls short: the layout does not change, and a control that already clears the floor answers for exactly its own box (under a mouse, that is every control). `touch-target-block` grows the block axis only, for two targets side by side in one control (a chip's label and its cross). A box that clips throws the pseudo-element away, and `data-ripple` clips its wave; such a control either takes `touch-size`, which raises the box itself, or — what the primitives do — carries `data-ripple="inner"` and paints its wave into a `[data-ripple-host]` first child that does the clipping instead. Under a finger a chip also takes 4dp of margin above and below, so an 8dp-gapped row wraps on a 48dp pitch and each 48dp target meets the next row's rather than covering it — the layout Compose's `minimumInteractiveComponentSize` reserves. `Chip` at 32dp and `Button size="xs"` keep their painted size; the target extends beyond the component bounds, as M3 states it. The same primitives carry `touch-manipulation` (no double-tap zoom on a pager or a like) and `select-none` (a long press on a label does not select it). See the control-height section for the two rules.

**A chip has one height, 32dp.** `AssistChipTokens.ContainerHeight` and `FilterChipTokens.ContainerHeight` are both 32 and the token set offers no second figure — there is nothing for a `size` prop to choose between.

**A drawer row's padding is asymmetric, and that is the spec's.** `NavigationDrawer.kt`'s item is `padding(start = 16.dp, end = 24.dp)` — the extra 8dp is there because a trailing element (an unread count) needs more air than a leading one. The asymmetry holds at both densities, because it is about the badge rather than the height. The row is a pill (`ActiveIndicatorShape`) at 48dp under a pointer and `ActiveIndicatorHeight`'s 56 under a finger, its icon is 24dp, the gaps either side of the label are 12dp, and the item is inset 12dp from the drawer's own edge (`NavigationDrawerItemDefaults.ItemPadding`).

The account block above those rows is **not** one of them. It is a two-line list item — avatar, name, supporting line — so it takes `ListTokens`: a 16dp corner (`ItemSelectedContainerShape`), 16dp leading, a 40dp avatar, and 64dp at every density against `ItemTwoLineContainerHeight`'s 72 (the step down argued above). It must not wear the rows' pill shape, which is how those rows mean "you are here". Its avatar still starts on the same 28dp leading column as every nav icon — the part that has to agree.

The **app bar does not join that column**: it is `px-4 sm:px-26` — 16px on a phone, 104px from `sm` up — breathing room around the brand rather than a derivation. The bar spans both the drawer and the content area and belongs to neither, so aligning it to either one's grid is a false precision.

The name is `title-m` rather than `ListTokens.ItemLabelTextFont`'s `body-large`, and that is a stated divergence: same 16px, at `TitleMedium`'s weight and its 1.5 leading instead of the prose role's 1.75. A name in a control is a label, and the 4px that returns is what lets the 64dp box keep M3's own vertical padding. **The 72 has two consumers** — this block and `/messages`' contact row — and they move together; moving one alone is a divergence with no scale behind it. The contact rail's collapsed width is derived from that row (row height + the list's 8px inset either side, so the row comes out square and the portrait centres itself), so it moves too: 80px / 88px.

`Button` ships `responsiveLabel` to collapse to a square icon button below `sm` — use it in any row that would otherwise overflow on a phone.

No press-shrink. M3 gives no size feedback on press; the state layer and the ripple carry it. `active:scale` utilities is not used anywhere and should not come back — besides being off-spec, a control mid-transform corrupts the rect the hero flight reads on press. Two named exceptions, both from a component's own token set rather than from taste: the **switch handle** grows 24 → 28dp on press (`md-switch`'s `PressedHandleWidth`) and the **slider handle** narrows 4 → 2dp. A handle is the one control whose whole job is to be gripped, and M3 gives it a grip cue; nothing else gets one.

## Scrolling

`<body>` is `overflow: hidden`. The page scroller is `<main class="app-scroller">` in `components/AppLayout.tsx`, reachable via `getAppScroller()`. `window.scrollTo` is a no-op — use `scrollAppToTop` / `scrollAppToElement`.

Every scroll container carries `.main-scrollbar` (page-level, reserves its gutter), `.popover-scrollbar` (overlays, no gutter) or `.scrollbar-hide` (a horizontally scrolled chip row or tab rail, where a visible bar is chrome the row cannot afford). Never leave one unstyled: the browser default is a different width and colour, and the app then appears to change its scrollbar as you navigate.

Never give a child of the scroller `min-h-dvh` — the scroller is already shorter than the viewport (header + margins), so it forces a permanent scrollbar. Use `min-h-full`.

### Vertical position has one owner

`lib/scrollMemory.ts`, mounted once in `AppLayout` as `<RouteScrollMemory>`, and **every `router.push`/`replace` and every `<Link>` in the app passes `scroll: false`**. That is not a per-call-site decision; a new one that forgets it is a bug, and the flag is only a suppression — the position is written explicitly.

**A traversal from a picture's overlay back to the page it covered keeps that page's own offset.** The page never unmounted (the overlay is a parallel route, and `[data-page-content]` is keyed on the background pathname, so it is the same node), and a close that nothing flies may just have moved it on purpose — the list turning to the picture last on screen (`revealInImageSequence`). Restoring the entry's offset from before the picture opened put /history's list back 1466px under its closing container and landed the container on the scroll. A remount (R12-017, a page-presentation `/pic/` going back) is a new node and still restores.

Five situations, and the page turn and the tab switch are one rule:

| a new pathname | jump to 0. A new page starts at its top |
| back / forward | restore what that history entry had |
| a page turn (`?page=`) | `Pagination` owns it, and it **positions rather than travels** — see below |
| a tab switch (`?tab=`) | `startTabTransition` / `TabPanesDriver` own it |
| Back / Forward between two searches (`/search?q=`) | /search owns it: it keeps its own offsets by the same entry key and restores one once that search's results are on screen (a picture closing back onto a search is the hero's, not this). It learns of the traversal from the entry it renders, never from `popstate`: React renders a transition started inside that event synchronously, so the router's traversal can commit before a listener of the screen's own has run |

The owner depends on `pathname` alone, so it does not run for a search-only change at all — which is stronger than testing for one. The image detail stays the hero subsystem's, guarded both by `heroOwnsScreen()` and by a `/pic/` test for a cold load.

**Next's own handler is off rather than merely overridden.** It does reach the app scroller (`layout-router.js` falls through to `domNode.scrollIntoView()`), but it is the one writer that cannot consult `heroOwnsScreen()`, it tests the segment's top against `document.documentElement.clientHeight` rather than the scroller's box, `block: 'start'` lands on the segment's top edge rather than on `scrollTop === 0`, it fires after the commit and so races `RouteCrossFade`, and it ends with `domNode.focus()`, moving focus to the route segment. `scroll: false` is also not a hard opt-out: `completeSoftNavigation` neutralises the *current* navigation's targets while leaving an earlier unconsumed `scrollRef` live.

**Offsets are keyed per history entry, not per URL** — `window.navigation.currentEntry.key`, with a URL fallback whose one observable difference is documented at the call site. Do not merge a key into `history.state`: the App Router rebuilds that object on every commit and drops anything it does not own, which `lib/hero/history.ts` already spends three mechanisms working around.

**A page turn glides on the distance law, like every other programmatic scroll, and there is no fixed-length escape hatch.** `scrollAppToElement` scales its tween with the square root of the travel (360–1100ms), so the *rate* is non-linear in the distance: a short hop is brisk, a long one takes its time. Hold the duration still and the speed rises with however far you happened to be scrolled.

**`npm run perf:pageturn` asserts the predicate, not the mechanism: no in-view card may be blank during a page turn, and none may be hidden after a cold load has painted it.** The lesson behind it, learned over three passes: when a transition reads wrong, check what is on screen during it before changing how long it lasts — the glide was blamed for blank travel that `FadeInImage`'s dropped placeholder caused, and fixing the placeholder is what made the honest duration affordable. Reaching for the duration or the easing is the mistake the report invites; both times the fault was which bitmap was on screen. Standing rules from the same stretch:

- `<StaggerGrid>` is a **sibling after** the grid it targets (a parent's ref attaches only after its children's layout effects run, so nesting it meant the mount pass found no root), and the hook **latches once per mount**: a page turn is a content replacement inside a grid that never left the screen — the motion carrying it is the glide, and a second entrance on top is the 动画重叠 failure. A breakpoint reflow no longer replays it either: a resize is not an arrival.
- **The cascade must not play on a cold load or compete with an active route entrance.** `/` renders its first feed page on the server, so on a cold load the cards are painted before the engine arrives and a late cascade would park visible cards at `autoAlpha: 0`. `StaggerGrid` decides **once, at mount**, whether the engine is already resident and never subscribes to its later arrival. Even with the engine resident, `useStaggerGridOn` checks the route's pre-commit marker before seeding any card transforms and latches the skip when that route owns the entrance. `warmMotion()` still fetches the engine on an idle callback for every other consumer.
- `FadeInImage`'s placeholder **cross-fades rather than unmounting**: the skeleton fades out on the image's own clock, released one `MOTION_SPEED_SCALE.slow`-scaled duration later — the wall-clock rule, because a timer written against the unscaled figure fires inside the motion it is meant to outlast.
- `runScroll` takes no length argument: a parameter with no call sites is one the next screen reaches for.

**The server cannot see which image line this device is on.** `resolveImageLine()` reads `localStorage` and the fetched route policy; in Node it falls back to the defaults and emits the proxy line for everybody — a visitor who had turned the image proxy off got a hydration mismatch on every card, and React does not patch mismatched attributes, so their preference was ignored for the whole first screen. `COOKIE_KEYS.imageLine` is the answer, in the same shape as `browsing`: `lib/route.ts` mirrors its own resolution into the cookie on every change, and `app/layout.tsx` hands it down through `ImageLineProvider` so the client's first render asks the same question. `createInitialAttempt` takes the line as an argument for that first attempt only; the failover ladder still reads the live answer, so a stale cookie costs one corrected attempt. (In production the first attempt is the optimizer on the raw URL, which no line touches, so only a browser-direct first attempt — development, an animated card — depends on it.) A first visit has no cookie and uses the defaults on both sides, and the cookie written during that load makes every load after it exact.

**The detail's picture is served as the CDN made it, not re-encoded.** `next.config.ts` sets `images.unoptimized` in development, so dev has always shown the untouched file; production put it through `/_next/image`, and at identical pixel dimensions (the optimizer clamps to the source) one real picture went 373,365 bytes → 170,826 at `q=82` (46%), 217,511 at `q=88` (58%) — less than half the bytes for the same resolution, plus **3–4 seconds of this server's CPU** per variant. `shouldBypassImageOptimization` covers Derpibooru's own derivatives now — `large`, `medium`, `small`, the thumbs — matched on the *raw* URL, since the image line may have wrapped it in a proxy's `?url=`. They are already size-appropriate files off a CDN; it gives up bytes (373KB against 171KB for an opened picture) and that is the right way round: the picture *is* the content here, and it now arrives in one hop. Gallery cards keep the optimizer, where a 308px card from an 800px source is a real saving — **handed the raw Derpibooru URL**, never a line-wrapped one: the optimizer runs on the server's network, and the worker and the CDN are open `?url=` proxies that `remotePatterns` therefore no longer allows (it pinned an open image proxy to this server). One optimizer attempt, then the visitor's line browser-direct, each retry on a backoff (`lib/imageLoader.ts`). An **animated** card skips the optimizer — it passes animations through unresized, so a card asked for the 800px `medium.gif` and got all 3.4MB — and takes the smallest Derpibooru rendition as wide as the card at up to 1.5× density (`pickRendition`). `sizes` comes from the layout's own numbers (`MASONRY_CARD_SIZES`: the docked drawer, the gutters, the column cap).

**The flight paints a `<canvas>` blitted from the gallery card's bitmap, and that — not the detail's `<img>` — is what flight sharpness is a property of.** `launchFlight` hands `createHeroFlight` the snapshot's `previewFrame`, which `captureHeroFrame` draws from the card's own `<img>`; `[data-image-detail-layer]` — preview and final alike — is the surface *underneath* it. In production the card is correctly sized *for a card* (304px from `/_next/image`) and the 944px flight box blows it up **3.11x**; in development the card holds 800px raw (`images.unoptimized`) and the same box is **1.18x**. So `warmedDetailFrames` (`lib/hero/media.ts`) rasterises a detail-sized source on the intent ladder and `prepareImageHero` hands **that** canvas to the flight.

**And the rung it warms is `medium`, not the `large` the detail displays.** Priced on the probe's shaped CDN at 10Mbps, `medium` is 110KB arriving in **132ms** where `large` is 370KB arriving in **344ms**; with the 70ms intent delay and a decode on top, large needs over 430ms of hover before the flight can use it and medium needs about 230ms. The flight warms the *smallest rung that is sharp enough*: 800px against the 944px well is **1.18x** — development's own figure. Those bytes are not speculative waste: the same URL becomes `previewSrc`, so the detail's preview layer paints it too (where it used to paint the card's 384px variant at 2.46x). One fetch, two consumers. The picture the user then looks at is still `large`, fetched by the final layer as always, landing at 0.74x. The `<img>` is dropped once the canvas exists, because retaining 24 decoded 1280x853 bitmaps is ~105MB; the canvas is already the unit `heroFrameCache` budgets in pixels. Animated sources are excluded rather than captured — a fresh decode is at frame 0, and taking off on a frame the user was not looking at is a visible jump.

**The cold tap is still the floor, and it is inherent.** `onPointerDown` starts the warm, which is the earliest honest moment (a touch screen has no hover, and pointerdown-to-click is ≥100ms) — but a tap with no hover cannot paint bytes that have not arrived, so frame 0 is the card's bitmap and the leg runs at 3.11x before the final layer lands sharp at ~430ms. Closing that would mean enlarging every card: measured through the optimizer at q=75 on three real pictures, the card's variant costs 62KB and the next step up 103KB — **+2MB on a fifty-card page** to improve the first 250ms of the few cards anyone opens. Not taken.

Probe rules that came out of that search, all load-bearing: measure image work at **DPR 2** — `npm run perf:sharpness` takes `PROBE_DPR`, and a fix that is perfect at DPR 1 can be inert on a phone. `scripts/probeHeroSharpness.mjs` must sample the flight's own canvas (`.image-hero-flyer-image`), not `[data-image-detail-layer]`, which answers "what will this look like once it has landed" and never "what does the flight look like"; `npm run perf:sharpness:warm` **asserts** the flight's canvas is bigger than the card's bitmap. `--throttle=<mbps>` shapes the faked CDN by delaying each fulfillment in proportion to the rung's weight and **must not be `Network.emulateNetworkConditions`** — a CDP-fulfilled response is synthesised inside the browser and is not subject to it, so it reports `transferSize: 0` and measures localhost; the rungs are padded to their real weights with a tEXt chunk. The probe's `--fake-cdn` answers the **browser's** derpicdn requests with a locally generated checkerboard PNG (checkerboard, because a flat image is sharp at every scale); it intercepts CDP, which cannot reach the Next server, so `/_next/image` still fetches through the real optimizer and the card-versus-flight comparison stays honest. The assertion is skipped without that flag, so a plain `npm run perf:sharpness` remains a report.
